Oh, no, it's actually relatively new. I hope that's okay. I used to be completely fine with them, honestly! I'd blow them up for the kids all the time without a second thought.

Well, I... I try not to let it affect my work too much, I really do. I don't want to be a burden on anyone else. But... lately, I guess I have been sort of... stepping back a little?

Or if there's a festival table set up with them nearby, I just sort of... casually find a reason to be on the other side of the room. I hope that doesn't make me sound like a terrible teacher. I really do love my job! 

And if I absolutely have to be near them, like if there's no way around it, I just try to keep my distance and... and focus really hard on just getting through the activity without looking directly at them. It's not too disruptive, I promise. Is it normal to try and work around it like that?

But ever since then, for the last seven months, I've just been a bit... jumpy around them. \n\nI know it sounds so silly to let one little pop bother me for so long, and I really should be over it by now. Does that make sense? I hope I'm explaining it right.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself trying to avoid situations or places where you might see balloons?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I try not to let it affect my work too much, I really do. I don't want to be a burden on anyone else. But... lately, I guess I have been sort of... stepping back a little? \n\nLike, if we have a birthday circle and parents bring them in, I might ask one of my aides to lead the songs for a bit. Or if there's a festival table set up with them nearby, I just sort of... casually find a reason to be on the other side of the room. I hope that doesn't make me sound like a terrible teacher. I really do love my job! \n\nAnd if I absolutely have to be near them, like if there's no way around it, I just try to keep my distance and... and focus really hard on just getting through the activity without looking directly at them. It's not too disruptive, I promise. Is it normal to try and work around it like that?\n\nDoctor: How much distress do you feel when you see a balloon and can't avoid it?\n\nPatient: Um, well... I really try to keep it together, especially for the kids. I don't want them to see me upset over something so small. But... if I'm being completely honest, it's pretty distressing. \n\nI know, logically, that a balloon in the classroom is probably just decoration, right? It's just a piece of rubber. But when I see one stretched tight, or... or bobbing near the children, it just feels like it could burst right in my face again. And I get... well, I get scared in a way that seems so much bigger than the situation actually warrants. \n\nI feel my heart racing, and I just want to run out of the room, which I know is so silly. I really hope you don't think I'm overreacting... it's just really hard to control in the moment. Does that make sense? I'm trying my best to handle it, I really am.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any other physical symptoms, like sweating or trembling, when you see a balloon and can't avoid it?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... not really? I mean, I definitely get that racing heart feeling I mentioned, but I don't think I'm, like, drenched in sweat or shaking uncontrollably or anything like that. I try really hard to just take deep breaths and stay composed. \n\nI might get a little bit tense, maybe? Just sort of bracing myself, I guess. But I promise I'm not falling apart in front of the children. I'm usually pretty good at keeping my hands steady if I need to help a student with something. I hope that's what you were asking? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right information.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, um... no, not really. I mean, a fire alarm is startling for anyone, right? Especially when you have a room full of five-year-olds to keep calm. But I don't get that same... that same intense, scary feeling like I do with the balloons. 

If a door slams or someone drops a heavy book, I might jump a little, just like a normal reaction, but then I'm fine. I can just brush it off and keep going with the lesson. It's really just the balloons that make me feel that way. 

I'm pretty good with regular noises, I promise. I wouldn't let something like a loud door disrupt the kids' day. Is it helpful to know that it's just that one specific thing that bothers me?

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, um... I think it's mostly the fear of what it might do. Like, I know a balloon in the classroom is probably just supposed to be a fun decoration. I really do know that! But... when I see one, especially if it looks stretched really tight, or if it's just bobbing around near the children... it just feels like it could burst right in my face at any second. 

And I get scared in a way that... well, I admit it seems a lot bigger than the situation actually calls for. I try so hard to hide it because I know it's a bit silly, and I would never want to ruin a celebration or make a big deal out of nothing in front of my students. I hope it doesn't make me sound completely unreasonable? I just want to make sure I'm explaining it the right way for you.

I can just brush it off and keep going with the lesson. It's really just the balloons that make me feel that way. \n\nI'm pretty good with regular noises, I promise. I wouldn't let something like a loud door disrupt the kids' day. Is it helpful to know that it's just that one specific thing that bothers me?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or fearful around other inflated objects, like pool toys or inflated beach balls?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no, those are completely fine! I actually use beach balls in class sometimes for, like, counting games or passing them around the circle. \n\nIt's really just the balloons. I think because a beach ball or a pool float, they're... they're sturdy, you know? They don't just pop out of nowhere with that awful sound. So I'm totally comfortable with those. I promise I don't have a problem with all toys or anything that would make my job harder. \n\nI hope that's okay? I just want to be clear that it's very contained to just the one thing. I'm really very capable with everything else the kids want to play with.\n\nDoctor: Is it the sight of the balloon itself that scares you, or is it more the fear of it popping that causes your distress?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... I think it's mostly the fear of what it might do. Like, I know a balloon in the classroom is probably just supposed to be a fun decoration. I really do know that! But... when I see one, especially if it looks stretched really tight, or if it's just bobbing around near the children... it just feels like it could burst right in my face at any second. \n\nAnd I get scared in a way that... well, I admit it seems a lot bigger than the situation actually calls for. I try so hard to hide it because I know it's a bit silly, and I would never want to ruin a celebration or make a big deal out of nothing in front of my students. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, no, it's definitely not the shape or the color. I mean, they're just... they're just colorful shapes, right? I actually think they look quite pretty when they're all set up for a party. 

It's really just the... the anticipation, I guess? The waiting for that awful noise. Like I was saying, it's just the popping that I worry about. I don't have any problem with the way they look, I promise. I wouldn't want you to think I was afraid of colors or circles or anything silly like that. 

Does that make sense? I hope I'm being clear that it's just the noise I'm afraid of, not the actual object itself.

Oh, um... you know, I don't think so? I mean, I don't really watch a lot of things with balloons popping in them, but if I did... I think I'd be okay.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, absolutely. Yes, definitely. If... if I knew for a hundred percent fact that they wouldn't pop, I wouldn't be scared of them at all. 

Like, if there was some sort of... I don't know, magic balloon that could never ever burst, I'd probably be the first one blowing them up for the kids' birthdays! It really is just that terrible anticipation of the bang. 

If you took the popping out of the equation, they'd just be fun decorations to me. I wouldn't have to worry about jumping out of my skin or scaring the students. Does that help clarify things? I hope I'm giving you the right kind of answers to help figure this out.

I just sort of... freeze for a moment before I can think clearly about what I’m actually supposed to be doing. I'm sorry if that sounds strange... is that the kind of thing you help people with? I just want to make sure I'm in the right place.\n\nDoctor: Have you always felt this way about balloons, or is this a relatively new reaction for you?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, it's actually relatively new. I hope that's okay. I used to be completely fine with them, honestly! I'd blow them up for the kids all the time without a second thought. \n\nBut... well, about seven months ago, we were having this lovely children's art celebration at the preschool. I was helping set up the decorations, and one just... it burst right next to my face. I mean, it wasn't a huge deal or anything, it just really, really startled me. But ever since then, for the last seven months, I've just been a bit... jumpy around them. \n\nI know it sounds so silly to let one little pop bother me for so long, and I really should be over it by now. Does that make sense? I hope I'm explaining it right.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself trying to avoid situations or places where you might see balloons?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I try not to let it affect my work too much, I really do. I don't want to be a burden on anyone else. Or if there's a festival table set up with them nearby, I just sort of... casually find a reason to be on the other side of the room. I hope that doesn't make me sound like a terrible teacher. I really do love my job! \n\nAnd if I absolutely have to be near them, like if there's no way around it, I just try to keep my distance and... and focus really hard on just getting through the activity without looking directly at them. It's not too disruptive, I promise. Is it normal to try and work around it like that?\n\nDoctor: How much distress do you feel when you see a balloon and can't avoid it?\n\nPatient: Um, well... I really try to keep it together, especially for the kids. I don't want them to see me upset over something so small. But... if I'm being completely honest, it's pretty distressing. \n\nI know, logically, that a balloon in the classroom is probably just decoration, right? It's just a piece of rubber. But when I see one stretched tight, or... or bobbing near the children, it just feels like it could burst right in my face again. And I get... well, I get scared in a way that seems so much bigger than the situation actually warrants. \n\nI feel my heart racing, and I just want to run out of the room, which I know is so silly. I really hope you don't think I'm overreacting... it's just really hard to control in the moment. Does that make sense? I'm trying my best to handle it, I really am.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any other physical symptoms, like sweating or trembling, when you see a balloon and can't avoid it?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... not really? I mean, I definitely get that racing heart feeling I mentioned, but I don't think I'm, like, drenched in sweat or shaking uncontrollably or anything like that. I try really hard to just take deep breaths and stay composed. \n\nI might get a little bit tense, maybe? Just sort of bracing myself, I guess. But I promise I'm not falling apart in front of the children. I'm usually pretty good at keeping my hands steady if I need to help a student with something. I hope that's what you were asking? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're having a panic attack when you see a balloon and can't avoid it?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no, I don't think so? I mean, I'm not entirely sure what qualifies as a real panic attack, but... I'm definitely not losing complete control or hyperventilating or anything scary like that. \n\nI really try to keep things as normal as possible. It's just that intense fear and the racing heart I mentioned, but I can still stand there and... and function, you know? I wouldn't let it get to the point where I'm having a total breakdown, especially not at work. I promise I'm holding it together better than that. \n\nI hope that makes sense? I'm really doing my best to manage it, so I wouldn't call it a panic attack. Is it a good sign that it hasn't gotten to that level?\n\nDoctor: Are you startled or frightened by other loud noises, like a door slamming or a fire alarm?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no, not really. I mean, a fire alarm is startling for anyone, right? Especially when you have a room full of five-year-olds to keep calm. But I don't get that same... that same intense, scary feeling like I do with the balloons. \n\nIf a door slams or someone drops a heavy book, I might jump a little, just like a normal reaction, but then I'm fine. I can just brush it off and keep going with the lesson. It's really just the balloons that make me feel that way. \n\nI'm pretty good with regular noises, I promise. I wouldn't let something like a loud door disrupt the kids' day. Is it helpful to know that it's just that one specific thing that bothers me?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or fearful around other inflated objects, like pool toys or inflated beach balls?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no, those are completely fine! I actually use beach balls in class sometimes for, like, counting games or passing them around the circle. \n\nIt's really just the balloons. I think because a beach ball or a pool float, they're... they're sturdy, you know? They don't just pop out of nowhere with that awful sound. So I'm totally comfortable with those. I promise I don't have a problem with all toys or anything that would make my job harder. \n\nI hope that's okay? I just want to be clear that it's very contained to just the one thing. I'm really very capable with everything else the kids want to play with.\n\nDoctor: Is it the sight of the balloon itself that scares you, or is it more the fear of it popping that causes your distress?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... I think it's mostly the fear of what it might do. Like, I know a balloon in the classroom is probably just supposed to be a fun decoration. I really do know that! But... when I see one, especially if it looks stretched really tight, or if it's just bobbing around near the children... it just feels like it could burst right in my face at any second. \n\nAnd I get scared in a way that... well, I admit it seems a lot bigger than the situation actually calls for. I'd blow them up for the kids all the time without a second thought. \n\nBut... well, about seven months ago, we were having this lovely children's art celebration at the preschool. I was helping set up the decorations, and one just... it burst right next to my face. I mean, it wasn't a huge deal or anything, it just really, really startled me. But ever since then, for the last seven months, I've just been a bit... jumpy around them. \n\nI know it sounds so silly to let one little pop bother me for so long, and I really should be over it by now. Does that make sense? I hope I'm explaining it right.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself trying to avoid situations or places where you might see balloons?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I try not to let it affect my work too much, I really do. I don't want to be a burden on anyone else. But... lately, I guess I have been sort of... stepping back a little? \n\nLike, if we have a birthday circle and parents bring them in, I might ask one of my aides to lead the songs for a bit. Or if there's a festival table set up with them nearby, I just sort of... casually find a reason to be on the other side of the room. I hope that doesn't make me sound like a terrible teacher. I really do love my job! \n\nAnd if I absolutely have to be near them, like if there's no way around it, I just try to keep my distance and... and focus really hard on just getting through the activity without looking directly at them. It's not too disruptive, I promise. Is it normal to try and work around it like that?\n\nDoctor: How much distress do you feel when you see a balloon and can't avoid it?\n\nPatient: Um, well... I really try to keep it together, especially for the kids. I don't want them to see me upset over something so small. But... if I'm being completely honest, it's pretty distressing. \n\nI know, logically, that a balloon in the classroom is probably just decoration, right? It's just a piece of rubber. But when I see one stretched tight, or... or bobbing near the children, it just feels like it could burst right in my face again. And I get... well, I get scared in a way that seems so much bigger than the situation actually warrants. \n\nI feel my heart racing, and I just want to run out of the room, which I know is so silly. I really hope you don't think I'm overreacting... it's just really hard to control in the moment. Does that make sense? I'm trying my best to handle it, I really am.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any other physical symptoms, like sweating or trembling, when you see a balloon and can't avoid it?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... not really? I mean, I definitely get that racing heart feeling I mentioned, but I don't think I'm, like, drenched in sweat or shaking uncontrollably or anything like that. I try really hard to just take deep breaths and stay composed. \n\nI might get a little bit tense, maybe? Just sort of bracing myself, I guess. But I promise I'm not falling apart in front of the children. I'm usually pretty good at keeping my hands steady if I need to help a student with something. I hope that's what you were asking? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're having a panic attack when you see a balloon and can't avoid it?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no, I don't think so? I mean, I'm not entirely sure what qualifies as a real panic attack, but... I'm definitely not losing complete control or hyperventilating or anything scary like that. \n\nI really try to keep things as normal as possible. It's just that intense fear and the racing heart I mentioned, but I can still stand there and... and function, you know? I wouldn't let it get to the point where I'm having a total breakdown, especially not at work. I promise I'm holding it together better than that. \n\nI hope that makes sense? I'm really doing my best to manage it, so I wouldn't call it a panic attack. Is it a good sign that it hasn't gotten to that level?\n\nDoctor: Are you startled or frightened by other loud noises, like a door slamming or a fire alarm?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no, not really. I mean, a fire alarm is startling for anyone, right? Especially when you have a room full of five-year-olds to keep calm. But I don't get that same... that same intense, scary feeling like I do with the balloons. \n\nIf a door slams or someone drops a heavy book, I might jump a little, just like a normal reaction, but then I'm fine. I can just brush it off and keep going with the lesson. It's really just the balloons that make me feel that way. \n\nI'm pretty good with regular noises, I promise. I wouldn't let something like a loud door disrupt the kids' day. Is it helpful to know that it's just that one specific thing that bothers me?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or fearful around other inflated objects, like pool toys or inflated beach balls?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no, those are completely fine! I actually use beach balls in class sometimes for, like, counting games or passing them around the circle. \n\nIt's really just the balloons. I think because a beach ball or a pool float, they're... they're sturdy, you know? They don't just pop out of nowhere with that awful sound. So I'm totally comfortable with those. I promise I don't have a problem with all toys or anything that would make my job harder. \n\nI hope that's okay? I just want to be clear that it's very contained to just the one thing. I'm really very capable with everything else the kids want to play with.\n\nDoctor: Is it the sight of the balloon itself that scares you, or is it more the fear of it popping that causes your distress?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... I think it's mostly the fear of what it might do. Like, I know a balloon in the classroom is probably just supposed to be a fun decoration. I really do know that! But... when I see one, especially if it looks stretched really tight, or if it's just bobbing around near the children... it just feels like it could burst right in my face at any second. \n\nAnd I get scared in a way that... well, I admit it seems a lot bigger than the situation actually calls for. I try so hard to hide it because I know it's a bit silly, and I would never want to ruin a celebration or make a big deal out of nothing in front of my students. I hope it doesn't make me sound completely unreasonable? I just want to make sure I'm explaining it the right way for you.\n\nDoctor: Is the shape or color of the balloon itself frightening to you, or is it what the balloon might do that scares you?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, it's definitely not the shape or the color. I mean, they're just... they're just colorful shapes, right? I actually think they look quite pretty when they're all set up for a party. \n\nIt's really just the... the anticipation, I guess? The waiting for that awful noise. Like I was saying, it's just the popping that I worry about. I don't have any problem with the way they look, I promise. I wouldn't want you to think I was afraid of colors or circles or anything silly like that. \n\nDoes that make sense? I hope I'm being clear that it's just the noise I'm afraid of, not the actual object itself.\n\nDoctor: Is it only the sound of a balloon popping that scares you, or is there something else about the balloon itself that contributes to your fear?\n\nPatient: Oh, um, no, there really isn't anything else. I just want to make sure I'm giving you exactly what you need and not making it sound more complicated than it actually is. I mean, I don't really watch a lot of things with balloons popping in them, but if I did... I think I'd be okay. \n\nBecause when it's on a screen, I know it's not real, right? Like, it's not actually in the room with me. The volume is controlled, and it can't suddenly burst near my face or startle the kids. So, no, I don't think a video would bother me much at all. \n\nI hope that doesn't make my fear seem even more ridiculous? Like I can handle it on TV but not in real life... I just want to be completely honest with you so you have all the right information. Am I explaining that okay?\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of balloons decrease if you knew for certain that they would not pop?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. Yes, definitely. I don't know, magic balloon that could never ever burst, I'd probably be the first one blowing them up for the kids' birthdays! It really is just that terrible anticipation of the bang. \n\nIf you took the popping out of the equation, they'd just be fun decorations to me. I wouldn't have to worry about jumping out of my skin or scaring the students. Does that help clarify things? I hope I'm giving you the right kind of answers to help figure this out.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel less scared of balloons if you were the one in control of popping them?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... you know, I've never really thought about that. The noise is just so sudden and loud, no matter who makes it happen.\n\nI'm sorry, I know that's kind of a wishy-washy answer. Oh, a sealed container? Um... wow, I've never actually been in a situation like that, so I'm not entirely sure. 

I suppose... if I knew for an absolute fact that even if it did pop, the sound couldn't reach me or it wouldn't be loud... I'd probably be okay? Or at least, I wouldn't act as silly about it. 

But just seeing it, you know, blown up and stretched out... I might still feel a tiny bit on edge, just out of pure habit. I know that probably sounds a bit irrational, I'm sorry. 

I feel terrible that I can't give you a straight yes or no! I just haven't ever tested that out or seen a balloon in a box like that. Am I still being helpful? I really want to make sure I'm giving you what you need for your assessment.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, um... intentionally popped? Like, if I was at some sort of... demonstration or something? 

I guess... I mean, if I knew it was supposed to happen, and I knew exactly when and how, it would probably be a lot easier to handle than a surprise at a birthday party. I could prepare myself, and I wouldn't have to spend the whole time wondering *if* it was going to happen. 

But... I don't know, even if it was controlled, just the thought of sitting there watching someone pop them on purpose... it still makes my stomach tie up in knots a little bit. I think I'd still be pretty uncomfortable, even if I tried really hard not to show it. 

I'm sorry, I feel like I keep giving you the same kind of answer! I really hope I'm not being frustrating. I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right picture of how this affects me. Is that okay?

I wouldn't let something like a loud door disrupt the kids' day. I'm really very capable with everything else the kids want to play with.\n\nDoctor: Is it the sight of the balloon itself that scares you, or is it more the fear of it popping that causes your distress?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... But... when I see one, especially if it looks stretched really tight, or if it's just bobbing around near the children... it just feels like it could burst right in my face at any second. \n\nAnd I get scared in a way that... well, I admit it seems a lot bigger than the situation actually calls for. I try so hard to hide it because I know it's a bit silly, and I would never want to ruin a celebration or make a big deal out of nothing in front of my students. I hope it doesn't make me sound completely unreasonable? I just want to make sure I'm explaining it the right way for you.\n\nDoctor: Is the shape or color of the balloon itself frightening to you, or is it what the balloon might do that scares you?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, it's definitely not the shape or the color.
